The great thing about this string notation is that it only takes seconds to check that the two strings on the right-hand side satisfy both conditions. Written out as integrals, these strings correspond to the following equality
6
6

6

Note the order of the correlation pairs is left ambiguous by the string notation, as for example the pairs
\langle 62\rangle
and
\langle 26\rangle
are denoted identically with string notation. However, as long as the ordering of each correlation term is consistent on both sides of the equation, the equality holds, as the integrand does not change when rearranging the integrals.
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{6}}dt_{5}\int_{0}^{t_{5}}dt_{4}\int_{0}^{t_{4}}dt_{3}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{3}}dt_{2}\int_{0}^{t_{2}}dt_{1}\,\mathcal{B}(\hat{1},\hat{2},\hat{3},\hat{4},\hat{5},\hat{6})\langle 62\rangle\langle 54\rangle\langle 31\rangle=

(176)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{6}}dt_{3}\int_{t_{3}}^{t_{6}}dt_{5}\int_{t_{3}}^{t_{5}}dt_{4}\int_{0}^{t_{3}}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{3}}dt_{2}\,\mathcal{B}(\hat{1},\hat{2},\hat{3},\hat{4},\hat{5},\hat{6})\langle 62\rangle\langle 54\rangle\langle 31\rangle

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{6}}dt_{1}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{6}}dt_{4}\int_{t_{4}}^{t_{6}}dt_{5}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{4}}dt_{3}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}dt_{2}\,\mathcal{B}(\hat{1},\hat{2},\hat{3},\hat{4},\hat{5},\hat{6})\langle 62\rangle\langle 54\rangle\langle 31\rangle.

We can then apply the Hadamard trick (Equation (
68
) three times for each integral expression on the right-hand side of this equation to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{6}}dt_{5}\int_{0}^{t_{5}}

\displaystyle dt_{4}\int_{0}^{t_{4}}dt_{3}\int_{0}^{t_{3}}dt_{2}\int_{0}^{t_{2}}dt_{1}\,\mathcal{B}(\hat{1},\hat{2},\hat{3},\hat{4},\hat{5},\hat{6})\langle 62\rangle\langle 54\rangle\langle 31\rangle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{6}}dt_{3}\int_{t_{3}}^{t_{6}}dt_{5}\,\mathcal{B}(\hat{3}\circ(\Gamma^{T}(t_{3})),\hat{6}\circ(\Gamma^{T}(t_{6})-\Gamma^{T}(t_{6}-t_{3})),\hat{3},\hat{5}\circ\Gamma^{T}(t_{5}-t_{3}),\hat{5},\hat{6})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{6}}dt_{1}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{6}}dt_{4}\,\mathcal{B}(\hat{1},\hat{6}\circ(\Gamma^{T}(t_{6})-\Gamma^{T}(t_{6}-t_{1})),\hat{1}\circ\Gamma(t_{4}-t_{1})),\hat{4},\hat{4}\circ\Gamma(t_{6}-t_{4}),\hat{6}).

We have reduced a quintuple integral into two double integrals.

The general algorithm follows naturally from this example. Start with a general TCL
2n
string:

0\,2n\kern 5.0pt\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2}\,\ldots\,\alpha_{n-1}\,|\,\alpha_{n}\,\ldots\,\alpha_{2n-1}.

(177)

We have omitted the Wick contraction pairings for the sake of clarity. We will instead explicitly state which digits are paired together by correlation terms. The general algorithm is as follows:

1.

Label
r
the rightmost
fixed
digit that is to the left of the vertical bar. Take the digit paired with
r
and call it
s.
Move
s
as far right as possible, without passing it through any fixed digits. Fix
s
at this position.

2.

Label
g_{s}
and
l_{s}
the greater and lesser children of
s
(in its newly fixed position), respectively. If neither of
g_{s}
or
l_{s}
are already fixed to the left of the vertical bar, then split
s
into the difference of two strings, the first with
s^{*}
in the place of
s,
and the second with
s_{*}
in the place of
s.
Otherwise, do not split
s.

We now introduce the label “
relevant child.
” The relevant child of
s^{*}
is
g_{s},
the relevant child of
s_{*}
is
l_{s},
and the relevant child of
s,
if the string has not been split, is the child of
s
that is not fixed to the left of the vertical bar. Label the relevant child of
s
as
c_{s}.

3.

Move
c_{s}
as far left as possible, without passing it through any fixed digits. Fix
c_{s}
at this position. You have reduced a TCL
2n
string into either one or two TCL
2n-2
string(s), each with some ancillary fixed digits.

4.

Repeat steps 1 through 3 on each of these strings (now with
n\rightarrow n-1
) until you are left with strings that only have one non-fixed digit (TCL
2
strings), at which point stop. You should have a sum of strings which all adhere to Condition 1 and Condition 2, and therefore the Hadamard trick can be performed on each of these strings
n
times systematically.

Some notes on the algorithm:

•

By the nature of the algorithm,
s
should never be fixed at the start of Step 1.

•

In Step 2, you may worry that we would have to split
s
if
g_{s}
and
l_{s}
are paired together. This is a valid concern, but, again by the nature of the algorithm, this can only occur if neither
g_{s}
nor
l_{s}
are fixed to the left of the vertical bar, so it is, logically speaking, a superfluous concern. This is because if, for example,
g_{s}
was fixed to the left of the vertical bar and
g_{s}
was paired with
l_{s},
then
l_{s}
would be fixed to the right of
s,
and therefore
l_{s}
could not be the lesser child of
s,
a contradiction.

•

Also in Step 2, if both children of
s
are already fixed to the left of the vertical bar, then
s
does not have a relevant child. In this case, you can choose any non-fixed digit as
c_{s}
. There might be some room for optimization here by choosing an ideal digit for
c_{s}
that would reduce the number of times we have to split our integrals after moving
c_{s}
in Step 3.

•

Be sure to account for the signs of each string via the distributive property when splitting strings.

•

Because this algorithm recursively splits each integral, the number of “Hadamardable” strings generated for an initial “seed” string grows exponentially with
n
for TCL
2n
(See Appendix
D
). Still, the cost of computing to find the integrals remains the bottleneck.
If
N
is the number of sample points, and
n
is the number of integrals, then the computational complexity grows as
\sim N^{n}
. The Hadamard trick cuts the number of integrals in half,
2n-1\to n-1
, reducing the computational complexity by a factor of
N^{n}
(which is huge). The number of integrals generated grows as
2^{n/2}
. So in total the complexity is divided by
(N/\sqrt{2})^{n}
, and since
N\gg\sqrt{2}
, the algorithm always provides exponential speedup for any n. This is very significant.

Appendix D
Estimation of the number of
n-1
dimensional integrals generated per
2n-1
dimensional integral

We can use a heuristic argument to approximate how many
n-1
dimensional integrals are generated from a single TCL
2n
expression. Equivalently, given a TCL
2n
string of the form given by Equation (
177
), what is the expected value of the number of “Hadamardable” strings we generated after completing the algorithm from Section
C.2
? We will call this the “multiplicity”,
m_{n},
of our algorithm.

The number of strings generated after the
k^{th}
recursion can be modeled as a branching process. Let
X_{n,j}
be a random variable which denotes the number of strings generated after the
j^{th}
recursion of our algorithm with initial seed a random TCL
2n
string. Denote

\mu_{n,j}=\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{X_{n,{j}}}{X_{n,j-1}}\right).

That is,
\mu_{n,j}
is the number of “offspring” generated in the
j^{th}
recursive step of our algorithm
per string in the
j-1^{th}
step of our algorithm.
It is the number of expected children we get per string in the
j^{th}
recursion of our algorithm. If we now start with a TCL
2n
string, the expected number of strings generated total after the
k^{th}
recursion is thus

q_{n,k}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{n,j},

and then the expected number of “Hadamardable” strings generated after completing the algorithm is

m_{n}\coloneqq q_{n,n}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{n,j},

(178)

since there are
n
recursive steps total. All we have to do is find the value of
\mu_{n,j}.

To find the value of
\mu_{n,j},
we just need to find the probability that we must split a random string at the
j^{th}
recursion. To do this, we count the number of fixed and non-fixed digits for a given string at the start of Step 2 of the
j^{th}
recursion of our algorithm. If we freeze one of our strings at this level of recursion, it will look roughly like this:

0\,2n\kern 5.0pt\overbracket{\beta_{1}\,\ldots\,\beta_{j-1}}^{j-1\text{ fixed digits}}\overbracket{\beta_{j}\,\ldots\,\beta_{n-1}|\,\beta_{n}\,\ldots\,\beta_{2n-j-1}}^{2n-2j\text{ non-fixed digits}}\,\overbracket{s\,\beta_{2n-j+1}\,\ldots\,\beta_{2n-1}.}^{j\text{ fixed digits}}.

We know
g_{s}
and
l_{s}
lie somewhere among
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots\beta_{2n-j-1}.
We will assume (without justification) that
g_{s}
and
l_{s}
are equally likely to be any one of these digits. Then the probability we need to split this string at this recursion is the probability that both the
g_{s}
and
l_{s}
are amongst the
2n-2j
non-fixed digits
7
7

7

We end up diving by 0 if
n=j=2
.
Otherwise, this expression is valid.
:

p_{n,j}\coloneqq\frac{2n-2j}{(2n-2j)+(j-1)}\cdot\frac{2n-2j-1}{(2n-2j)+(j-1)-1}=\frac{(2n-2j)(2n-2j-1)}{(2n-j-1)(2n-j-2)}.

We then have

\mu_{n,j}=1\cdot(1-p_{n,j})+2\cdot p_{n,j}=1+p_{n,j},

and then from Equation (
178
), the multiplicity of our algorithm is given by

m_{n}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(1+\frac{(2n-2j)(2n-2j-1)}{(2n-j-1)(2n-j-2)}\right),

(179)

valid for
n\geq 3
(see Footnote
7
).
Plotting
(n,\log_{2}(m_{n}))
for
3\leq n\leq 200,
and performing a linear regression, we find that
m_{n}
grows as

m_{n}\sim 2^{-0.133685}\cdot 2^{0.506474n}\approx 0.911\cdot 1.42^{n}.

Hence, to a good approximation,
m_{n}
grows with
n
as
2^{n/2}.
This is exponentially better than a worst case of
2^{n},
but still, the number of terms grows exponentially with
n.

Appendix E
Proof of the identity
107

Let us evaluate the integral

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{n}}dt_{n-1}\int_{0}^{t_{n-1}}dt_{n-2}\dots\int_{0}^{t_{2}}dt_{1}[\Gamma_{\omega}(t_{n})-\Gamma_{\omega}(t_{n}-t_{1})].

(180)

For the string
169
, we use

0\,n\,1\,2\,\ldots\,n-1,

(181)

and change the order following the algorithm described in appendix
B
, resulting in

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{n}}dt_{1}[\Gamma_{\omega}(t_{n})-\Gamma_{\omega}(t_{n}-t_{1})]\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{n}}dt_{2}\dots\int_{t_{n-2}}^{t_{n}}dt_{n-1}=\int_{0}^{t_{n}}dt_{1}\frac{(t_{n}-t_{1})^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}[\Gamma_{\omega}(t)-\Gamma_{\omega}(t_{n}-t_{1})]

(182)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n-2)!}\int_{0}^{t_{n}}dt_{1}\,t_{1}^{n-2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{n}}C(\tau)e^{i\omega\tau}=\frac{1}{(n-2)!}\int_{0}^{t_{n}}d\tau\,C(\tau)e^{i\omega\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}dt_{1}t_{1}^{n-2}

(183)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{t_{n}}d\tau\,\tau^{n-1}C(\tau)e^{i\omega\tau}=\frac{(-i)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\frac{\partial^{n-1}}{\partial\omega^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{t_{n}}d\tau C(\tau)e^{i\omega\tau}=\frac{(-i)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\frac{\partial^{n-1}\Gamma_{\omega}(t)}{\partial\omega^{n-1}}.

(184)

Appendix F
Complete TCL
6
-generator

The cumulative correlation terms were calculated by un-nesting the commutator of the Liouvillians, applying Wick’s theorem, and re-nesting the operators to reduce the number of terms. Further details are accessible on the https://github.com/A-Beta-in-Iceland/TCL. The terms were derived by scripts and human labor, resulting in agreement.

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},t_{b}-t\right)\right),\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\,\left[\hat{a},\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\,\hat{b}

(F109)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},t_{b}-t\right)\right),\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\,\left[\hat{a},\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\,\hat{b}

(F110)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},t_{b}-t\right)\right),\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a}\right)\right)\,\left[\hat{a},\hat{b}\right]

(F111)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},t_{b}-t\right)\right),\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\right]\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{a},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left[\hat{a},\hat{b}\right]

(F112)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},t_{b}-t\right)\right),\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},-t_{a}\right)\right)\,\left[\hat{a},\hat{b}\right]

(F117)

(F118)

(F138)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{b},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]

(F139)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{b},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]

(F140)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{b},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{a}\right)\right)\,\hat{b}\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]

(F141)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{b},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\hat{b}\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]

(F142)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{b},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t-t_{b},t-t_{a}\right)\right)\,\hat{b}\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]

(F143)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{b},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\hat{\varrho}\right]\right]

(F144)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{b},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\hat{b}\,\hat{\varrho}\right]\right]

(F145)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b},-t_{b}\right)\right),\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\hat{\varrho}\right]\right]

(F146)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b},-t_{b}\right)\right),\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\left[\hat{a},\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\right]

(F147)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b},-t_{b}\right)\right),\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\left[\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{a}\right)\right),\hat{a}\,\hat{\varrho}\right]\right]

(F148)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b},-t_{b}\right)\right),\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\left[\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{a}-t\right)\right),\hat{\varrho}\,\hat{a}\right]\right]

(F149)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{a},t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\hat{\varrho}\right]\right]

(F150)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\left[\hat{a},\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\right]

(F151)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{a}-t_{b}\right)\right),\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\,\left[\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t-t_{a}\right)\right),\hat{a}\,\hat{\varrho}\right]\right]

(F152)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},t_{b}-t\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{b}\right)\right)\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},-t_{a}\right)\right)\,\hat{b}\right]

(F153)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},t_{b}-t\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t-t_{b},t-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\,\hat{\varrho}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Delta\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},-t_{a}\right)\right)\,\hat{b}\right]

(F154)

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\hat{b}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a},t_{b}-t\right)\right),\left[\hat{a},\left(\hat{0}\circ\Delta\Gamma^{T}\!\left(t,t-t_{a}\right)\right)\,\hat{\varrho}\,\hat{b}\,\left(\hat{a}\circ\Gamma\!\left(t_{b}-t_{a}\right)\right)\right]\right]

(F155)

\displaystyle\Bigr)\Bigr]+\mathrm{h.c.}

(F156)

Numerical consistency check

For a
d
-level dynamical map
\Phi_{t}^{(n)}
propagated with the TCL
{}_{2\text{n}}
master equation, define the normalized Choi matrix and its negativity by the sum of the negative eigenvalues

\displaystyle J^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}|i\rangle\langle j|\otimes\Phi_{t}^{(n)}\!\left(|i\rangle\langle j|\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{C}^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\lambda_{k}[J^{(n)}(t)]<0}\lambda_{k}[J^{(n)}(t)].

(185)

An exactly completely positive map has
\mathcal{N}_{C}=0
. Choi negativity therefore quantifies deviations from complete positivity and, consequently, the accuracy with which the master equation approximates the dynamics.

We use a fixed four-level realization with energies
(-0.6490,-0.2045,0.1274,0.6317)
and a Hermitian Gaussian-unitary-ensemble coupling normalized to unit Frobenius norm. The zero-temperature Ohmic bath correlation is

C(t)=\frac{\alpha\omega_{c}^{2}}{2(1+i\omega_{c}t)^{2}},\qquad\omega_{c}=10.

(186)

The map is propagated without eigenvalue clipping or positivity repair over

0\leq t\leq 200
, using map spacing
0.0125
. The cumulative generators are

K^{[2]}=\mathcal{L}^{2}
,

K^{[4]}=\mathcal{L}^{2}+\mathcal{L}^{4}
, and

K_{\mathrm{corr}}^{[6]}=\mathcal{L}^{2}+\mathcal{L}^{4}+\mathcal{L}^{6}
.
To test cancellations within the sixth-order sum (terms F32–F155), eleven
separate calculations also propagate

K_{(-q)}^{[6]}=K_{\mathrm{corr}}^{[6]}-K_{6,q},\qquad q\in\mathcal{S}_{F},

(187)

where
\mathcal{S}_{F}
contains Eqs. (F32)–(F37), (F43), (F98), (F106),
(F115), and (F144) of the corrected replacement below. Each calculation
deletes the entire contribution shown in the indicated equation—including
its coefficient, common outer commutator, and Hermitian conjugate—while
leaving all other terms and model parameters unchanged. Removing even a
single one of these terms substantially increases the Choi negativity,
demonstrating the sensitivity of the result to cancellations within the
TCL
6
generator and providing a stringent numerical validation of its
implementation.

At
\alpha=0.03
, the corrected TCL
6
maximum Choi negativity in Fig.
8
is more than
five
orders of magnitude below TCL
4
and more than
nine
orders below TCL
2
. Figure
9
(d) shows the eleven one-term deletion results across the coupling sweep. Away from the numerical floor, removing a term generally increases the maximum Choi negativity. This behavior is consistent with cancellations among the sixth-order contributions: omitting one term changes their balance and increases the propagated map’s departure from complete positivity. Together with the order-by-order reduction in Fig.
8
, the deletion tests provide a numerical consistency check on the corrected expression for this model. A separate manuscript describing an accelerated method for evaluating TCL
6
is in preparation.

Figure 8:
Order-by-order Choi-negativity comparison for cumulative TCL
2
, TCL
4
, and corrected TCL
6
. Symbols show
\max_{0\leq t\leq 200}\mathcal{N}_{C}(t)
versus bath strength
\alpha
. Dashed lines are least-squares fits to
A\alpha^{p}
, with
p
reported in the legend. The TCL
2
and TCL
4
fits use
10^{-3}\leq\alpha\leq 10^{-1}
, while the TCL
6
fit uses
0.035\leq\alpha\leq 0.1
. At lower coupling, the smallest TCL
6
maxima lie at the numerical Choi-eigenvalue floor; a resolved early-time maximum appears near
\alpha=0.027
, and the dominant maximum switches to a late-time branch near
\alpha=0.0333
.

Figure 9:
Choi negativity for the complete corrected TCL
6
generator and eleven one-term deletions. The black curve uses all 124 collected terms; each colored curve omits the contribution named in the legend, including its coefficient, common outer commutator, and Hermitian conjugate. Panels (a)–(c) show
0\leq t\leq 200
,
0\leq t\leq 10
, and
150\leq t\leq 200
, respectively, at
\alpha=0.03
; panel (d) shows the maximum Choi negativity versus bath strength. The complete result has maximum
2.501\times 10^{-12}
at
t=1.0375
and is zero at every sampled time in panel (c).

Appendix G
Classification of
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}=0
TCL
4
Terms

The analysis starts with the distilled TCL
4
-generator, as established in Ref.
[
20
]
. For one liner-harmonic bath, the generator is simplified as:

\displaystyle L_{nm,ij}^{4}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b,c}A_{na}A_{ab}A_{bc}A_{ci}\delta_{jm}[\mathsf{F}_{cb,ci,ac}(t)-\mathsf{R}_{cb,ab,bi}(t)-\mathsf{F}_{ba,ci,ac}(t)+\mathsf{R}_{ic,ab,bi}(t)]

(188)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b}\Big\{A_{na}A_{ab}A_{bi}A_{jm}[-\mathsf{F}_{ba,bi,nb}(t)+\mathsf{R}_{ba,na,ai}(t)+\mathsf{F}_{an,bi,nb}(t)-\mathsf{R}_{ib,na,ai}(t)]

(189)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle A_{na}A_{ab}A_{bi}A_{jm}[\mathsf{C}_{ba,jm,ai}(t)+\mathsf{R}_{ba,jm,ai}(t)-\mathsf{C}_{ib,jm,ai}(t)-\mathsf{R}_{ib,jm,ai}(t)]

(190)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle A_{na}A_{ai}A_{jb}A_{bm}[-\mathsf{C}_{an,jb,ni}(t)-\mathsf{R}_{an,jb,ni}(t)+\mathsf{C}_{ia,jb,ni}(t)+\mathsf{R}_{ia,jb,ni}(t)]\Big\}+r.h.c.

(191)

Here we introduced the bath spectral density functions

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathsf{F}}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)+i\Gamma_{\omega_{2}}^{T}(t)\frac{\Gamma_{-\omega_{2}-\omega_{3}}(t)-\Gamma_{\omega_{1}}(t)}{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}}

(192)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)+i\Gamma_{\omega_{2}}^{\star}(t)\frac{\Gamma_{-\omega_{2}-\omega_{3}}(t)-\Gamma_{\omega_{1}}(t)}{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}}

(193)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathsf{R}}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)+i\Gamma_{\omega_{2}}(t)\frac{\Gamma_{-\omega_{2}-\omega_{3}}(t)-\Gamma_{\omega_{1}}(t)}{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}},

(194)

which display the following irreducible integrals:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{F}}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\Delta\Gamma_{\omega_{1}}(t,\tau)\Delta\Gamma_{\omega_{2}}^{T}(t,t-\tau)e^{-i(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})\tau}

(195)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\Delta\Gamma_{\omega_{1}}(t,\tau)\Delta\Gamma_{\omega_{2}}^{\star}(t,t-\tau)e^{-i(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})\tau}

(196)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{R}}_{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\Delta\Gamma_{\omega_{1}}(t,\tau)\Delta\Gamma_{\omega_{2}}(t,\tau)e^{-i(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})\tau}.

(197)

They are irreducible in a sense that they introduce new functions, which cannot be analytically expressed in terms of the BCF or
\Gamma
.
Given that
\tilde{\mathsf{F}}
,
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}
, and
\tilde{\mathsf{R}}
incorporate two correlation functions, their growths are suppressed relative to the algebraic terms in Eqs.
192
-
194
, which are in the growth algebra of Sec.
5.3
. The suppression of growth in
\tilde{\mathsf{F}}
,
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}
, and
\tilde{\mathsf{R}}
was analyzed in Ref.
[
20
]
and further discussion of such terms will not be pursued, as they are subleading. However, they may need to be revisited for other resummations in future.

When the sum
\omega_{\Sigma}=\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}
in the algebraic terms in
192
-
194
equals zero, it becomes essential to examine the limit
\omega_{\Sigma}\to 0
, which introduces the derivative
\partial\Gamma_{\omega}(t)/\partial\omega
.
The analysis proceeds by imposing the condition
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}=0
across the sixteen instances of
\mathsf{F}
,
\mathsf{C}
, or
\mathsf{R}
in the equations
188
-
191
, followed by the collection and organization of the resulting terms.

Take the initial occurrence of
\mathsf{F}_{cb,ci,ac}(t)
found on line
188
.
The condition being enforced is
\omega_{cb}+\omega_{ci}+\omega_{ac}=\omega_{cb}+\omega_{ai}=0
.
Two sets of indices exist: (
c=b
and
a=i
) or (
c=i
and
b=a
). The intersections of the sets must be counted once.
The resulting growth term is

\displaystyle\delta_{jm}\sum_{b}(A_{ni}|A_{ib}|^{2}A_{bb}\mathsf{F}_{bb,bi,ib}+A_{nb}A_{bi}A_{bb}A_{ii}\mathsf{F}_{ib,ii,bi})

(198)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\delta_{jm}\sum_{b}\big(A_{nb}A_{bi}A_{bb}A_{ii}\Gamma_{0}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{ib}}+A_{ni}|A_{ib}|^{2}A_{bb}\Gamma_{ib}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\omega=0}\big).

(199)

All the derivatives from line
188
and
189
result in the net contribution of

\displaystyle\text{Line}\penalty\ \ref{Eq:ExplicitLiouv}+\ref{Eq:Expl1}

\displaystyle=i\delta_{jm}\sum_{k}A_{nk}A_{ki}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{ik}}\sum_{c}\big[|A_{ic}|^{2}\Gamma_{ic}-|A_{kc}|^{2}\Gamma_{kc}\big]

(200)

\displaystyle-iA_{ni}A_{jm}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{in}}\sum_{c}\big[|A_{ic}|^{2}\Gamma_{ic}-|A_{nc}|^{2}\Gamma_{nc}\big].

(201)

We follow up with the similar analysis in the subsequent lines, which will include a larger variety of terms:

Line

190

\displaystyle=\delta_{jm}\sum_{k}A_{nk}A_{ki}A_{jj}(A_{kk}-A_{ii})2iJ_{0}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{ik}}

(202)

\displaystyle-\delta_{ij}|A_{im}|^{2}\sum_{k}A_{nk}A_{km}2iJ_{im}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{mk}}

(203)

\displaystyle+A_{nm}A_{ji}|A_{jm}|^{2}2iJ_{jm}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{ij}}

(204)

Line

191

\displaystyle=-A_{ni}A_{jm}A_{jj}(A_{nn}-A_{ii})2iJ_{0}(t)\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{in}}

(205)

\displaystyle+\,\delta_{ij}\sum_{k}|A_{ik}|^{2}A_{nk}A_{km}2iJ_{ik}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{kn}}

(206)

\displaystyle-|A_{jn}|^{2}A_{nm}A_{ji}2iJ_{jn}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{ij}}.

(207)

Reflecting on the Bloch-Redfield generator in Eq.
71
,

L^{2}_{nm,ij}(t)=A_{ni}A_{jm}\Gamma_{in}(t)-\sum_{k=1}^{N}A_{nk}A_{ki}\Gamma_{ik}\delta_{jm}+r.h.c.
, allows us to incorporate Eqs.
202
and
205

into Eqs.
200
and

201
, respectively. The result is a shift in the spectral density

\displaystyle\Gamma_{ik}

\displaystyle\mapsto\Gamma_{ik}-i\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{ik}}\big[\sum_{c}(|A_{ic}|^{2}\Gamma_{ic}-|A_{kc}|^{2}\Gamma_{kc})+2J_{0}(t)A_{jj}(A_{kk}-A_{ii})\big].

(208)

The remaining terms are not embeddable, as indicated in lines
203
,
204
,
206
, and
207
. The nonembeddable terms have the following Liouvillian:

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{nm,ij}^{2}

\displaystyle=-2i\delta_{ij}\sum_{k}A_{nk}A_{km}\bigg(|A_{im}|^{2}J_{im}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{mk}}-|A_{ik}|^{2}J_{ik}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{kn}}\bigg)

(209)

\displaystyle-2iA_{nm}A_{ji}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{ij}}(|A_{jn}|^{2}J_{jn}-|A_{jm}|^{2}J_{jm})+r.h.c.

(210)

For one qubit, we prevent double or triple counting using symbolic math, leading to
\tilde{L}_{nm,ij}^{2}=

\displaystyle 2i|A_{12}|^{2}\begin{pmatrix}|A_{12}|^{2}J_{12}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{21}}&0&0&-|A_{12}|^{2}J_{21}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{12}}\\
A_{21}(A_{22}-A_{11})J_{12}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{11}}&-|A_{12}|^{2}J_{12}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{21}}&A_{21}^{2}J_{21}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{12}}&A_{21}(A_{11}-A_{22})J_{21}(\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{12}}-\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{11}})\\
A_{12}(A_{11}-A_{22})J_{12}(\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{11}}-\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{21}})&A_{12}^{2}J_{12}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{21}}&-|A_{21}|^{2}J_{21}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{12}}&A_{12}(A_{11}-A_{22})J_{21}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{11}}\\
-|A_{12}|^{2}J_{12}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{21}}&0&0&|A_{12}|^{2}J_{21}\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial\omega_{12}}\end{pmatrix}

(211)

+r.h.c.
.

Appendix H
Return to Equilibrium

In this appendix we compute the leading correction to the steady states of the rTCL
4
-ME.
We utilize the perturbation theory of the asymptotic states, as outlined in Refs.
[
38
,
40
,
20
]
. If the asymptotic generator in the Schrödinger picture is expressed as

L=L^{0}+L^{2}+L^{4},

(212)

where
L^{2k}
is proportional to
\lambda^{2k}
, then the correction to the steady population of the qubit, to precision
O(\lambda^{2})
, is obtained using
[
20
, Eqs. 71 and 75]
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k}L^{2}_{nn,kk}\rho_{kk}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k}L^{4}_{nn,kk}\rho_{kk}^{0}-\sum_{l\neq m}L^{2}_{nn,lm}\rho_{lm}^{2}

(213)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k}L^{4}_{nn,kk}\rho_{kk}^{0}+i\sum_{k}\sum_{l\neq m}\frac{L^{2}_{nn,lm}L^{2}_{lm,kk}}{\omega_{lm}}\rho_{kk}^{0}.

(214)

Here,
\rho_{kk}^{0}
are the zeroth-order thermal populations of the system, and
\rho_{lm}^{2}
are the coherences computed in quadratic precision.

From the Bloch-Redfield generator in Eq.
71
, we find

L^{2}_{nn,kk}=2|A_{nk}|^{2}J_{kn}-2\delta_{kn}\sum_{q}|A_{nq}|^{2}J_{nq}
, so that
L^{2}_{22,22}=-2|A_{12}|^{2}J_{\beta,\Delta}
and
L^{2}_{22,11}=2|A_{12}|^{2}J_{\beta,-\Delta}
. In the unbiased SBM, which we shall consider from now on, unless stated otherwise, the second order generator has neither population-to-coherence nor coherence-to-population transfer matrix elements, so the second terms on the right-hand-sides on line
213
and
214
are zero. Moreover, for the qubit we have
\rho_{11}^{2}+\rho_{22}^{2}=0
, leading to the correction

\displaystyle\rho_{22}^{2}=-\frac{\sum_{k}L_{22,kk}^{4}\rho_{kk}^{0}}{L_{22,22}^{2}-L_{22,11}^{2}}=\frac{\sum_{k}L_{22,kk}^{4}\rho_{kk}^{0}}{2|A_{12}|^{2}(J_{\beta,\Delta}+J_{\beta,-\Delta})}.

(215)

First, we examine the effect of the embeddable terms, which have the Liouvillian

L^{4}
in the same form as
L^{2}
but with the spectral density
J_{\beta,\pm\Delta}
replaced by the shift
\delta J_{\beta,\pm\Delta}
obtained from Eq.
208
. That is,
L^{4}_{22,22}=-2|A_{12}|^{2}\delta J_{\beta,\Delta}
,
L^{4}_{22,11}=2|A_{12}|^{2}\delta J_{\beta,-\Delta}
, and

\displaystyle\delta J_{\beta,\pm\Delta}

\displaystyle=\text{Re}\Big[-i\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\pm\Delta}|A_{12}|^{2}(\Gamma_{\beta,\pm\Delta}-\Gamma_{\beta,\mp\Delta})\Big]

(216)

\displaystyle=\pm\Big(\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\pm\Delta}\nu_{2}+\frac{\partial J_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\pm\Delta}\tilde{\omega}_{\beta,21}\Big).

(217)

Inserting into Eq.
215
, and using
\rho_{11}^{0}=1-\rho_{22}^{0}=J_{\beta,\Delta}/(J_{\beta,\Delta}+J_{\beta,-\Delta})
, we obtain

\rho_{22}^{2}=-\nu_{2}\frac{J_{\beta,\Delta}\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\big|_{-\Delta}+J_{\beta,-\Delta}\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\big|_{\Delta}}{(J_{\beta,-\Delta}+J_{\beta,\Delta})^{2}}-\tilde{\omega}_{\beta,21}\frac{J_{\beta,\Delta}\frac{\partial J_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\big|_{-\Delta}+J_{\beta,-\Delta}\frac{\partial J_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\big|_{\Delta}}{(J_{\beta,-\Delta}+J_{\beta,\Delta})^{2}}.

(218)

Applying the KMS conditions
J_{\beta,\Delta}=e^{\beta\Delta}J_{\beta,-\Delta}
and
J_{\beta,\Delta}^{\prime}=\beta e^{\beta\Delta}J_{\beta,-\Delta}-e^{\beta\Delta}J_{\beta,-\Delta}^{\prime}
, where the prime indicates derivative over frequency, we find

\rho_{22}^{2}=-\frac{\nu_{2}}{J_{\beta,\Delta}+J_{\beta,-\Delta}}\Big[\rho_{11}^{0}\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\big|_{-\Delta}+\rho_{22}^{0}\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\big|_{\Delta}\Big]-\beta\rho_{11}^{0}\rho_{22}^{0}\tilde{\omega}_{\beta,21}

(219)

Subsequently, utilizing
\nu_{2}=|A_{12}|^{2}(J_{\beta,\Delta}-J_{\beta,-\Delta})
and

\tilde{\omega}_{\beta,21}=|A_{12}|^{2}(S_{\beta,\Delta}-S_{\beta,-\Delta})
,
the population of the excited state becomes

\displaystyle\rho_{22}

\displaystyle=\rho_{22}^{0}-|A_{12}|^{2}\Big[\tanh\,\frac{\beta\Delta}{2}\Big(\rho_{11}^{0}\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{-\Delta}+\rho_{22}^{0}\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\Delta}\Big)

(220)

\displaystyle+\beta\rho_{11}^{0}\rho_{22}^{0}\big(S_{\beta,\Delta}-S_{\beta,-\Delta}\big)\Big].

(221)

Let us now compute the contribution from the non-emebeddable terms. From Eq.
211
, we have
\tilde{L}^{2}_{22,22}=-4|A_{12}|^{4}J_{\beta,\Delta}\partial S_{\beta,\omega}/{\partial\omega}|_{-\Delta}
and

\tilde{L}^{2}_{22,11}=4|A_{12}|^{4}J_{\beta,-\Delta}\partial S_{\beta,\omega}/{\partial\omega}|_{\Delta}
.
Inserting into Eq.
215
, we obtain the contribution

\tilde{\rho}_{22}^{2}=2|A_{12}|^{2}\frac{J_{\beta,\Delta}J_{\beta,-\Delta}}{(J_{\beta,\Delta}+J_{\beta,-\Delta})^{2}}\Big(\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\big|_{\Delta}-\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\big|_{-\Delta}\Big).

(222)

Adding to the previous equation, we obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{22}

\displaystyle=\rho_{22}^{0}+|A_{12}|^{2}\Big[\Big(-\rho_{11}^{0}\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{-\Delta}+\rho_{22}^{0}\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\Delta}\Big)

(223)

\displaystyle-\beta\rho_{11}^{0}\rho_{22}^{0}\big(S_{\beta,\Delta}-S_{\beta,-\Delta}\big)\Big].

(224)

Let us transform this following the next equalities:

\displaystyle S_{\beta,\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}P\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega^{\prime}\,\frac{J_{\beta,\omega^{\prime}}}{\omega-\omega^{\prime}}

(225)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}P\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega^{\prime}\,\frac{J_{\omega^{\prime}}}{\omega^{2}-\omega^{\prime 2}}\big(\omega\coth\,\frac{\beta\omega^{\prime}}{2}+\omega^{\prime}\big)

(226)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial S_{\beta,\omega}}{\partial\omega}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}P\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega^{\prime}\,\frac{J_{\omega^{\prime}}}{(\omega^{2}-\omega^{\prime 2})^{2}}\big[(\omega^{2}+\omega^{\prime 2})\coth\,\frac{\beta\omega^{\prime}}{2}+2\omega\omega^{\prime}\big]

(227)

With some algebra, we arrive at

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{22}&=\rho_{22}^{0}+\frac{|A_{12}|^{2}}{\pi}P\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega^{\prime}\,\frac{J_{\omega^{\prime}}}{(\Delta^{2}-\omega^{\prime 2})^{2}}\Big\{\coth\,\frac{\beta\omega^{\prime}}{2}\,\big[(\Delta^{2}+\omega^{\prime 2})\tanh\,\frac{\beta\Delta}{2}\\
&+\frac{\beta\Delta}{2}(\omega^{\prime 2}-\Delta^{2})\text{sech}^{2}\,\frac{\beta\Delta}{2}\big]-2\Delta\omega^{\prime}\Big\},\end{split}

(228)

which is precisely the same as the mean-force Gibbs state correction
[
96
,
87
]
.

We have also computed the asymptotic state of the TCL
4
master equation
at arbitrary temperature, by substituting the TCL
4
generator into Eq.
212
, and find that is is the same as
228
, within at least eight significant digits. This is a remarkable result, because the quartic component of the rTCL
4
generator is a subset
of the TCL
4
generator terms, picked based on the leading late-time growths. These two tensors have different population-to-population matrix elements, and the relaxation rates of rTCL
4
and TCL
4
can be different by more than 10%.
Nevertheless, they have the same asymptotic states. This exemplifies that canonical consistency can be achieved using different master equations. Since TCL
4
is the complete fourth order generator, and rTCL
4
is only a subset, it follows that enforcing canonical consistency does not guarantee accuracy at all time scale, even though the asymptotic states are identical.

We have examined larger systems, with three or four energy levels, and in those systems the rTCL
4
master equation does not lead to return to equilibrium at nonzero temperature to quadratic precision (e.g., it returns to equilibrium only to precision
O(\lambda^{0})
like the Davies or TCL
2
-Bloch-Redfield ME). Whereas, we find that the TCL
4
generator always generates to the mean-force Gibbs state to precision
O(\lambda^{2})
within the numerical precision. As in our previous work,
[
20
]
,
the return to equilibrium in TCL
4
is proven only numerically, but the reasons for the agreement in arbitrary systems remains a puzzle. Recently, the analytic proof for any qubit (including the biased regime) and in a bath with analytic continuity of the spectral functions in the entire upper complex plane was accomplished in Ref.
[
97
]
.

In the presence of dephasing, the asymptotic populations of TCL
4
remain the same as those given by Eq.
228
, in agreement with the mean-force Gibbs state, but the rTCL
4
no longer leads to the equilibrium state to precision
O(\lambda^{4})
.
To conclude, at zero temperature, all rTCL, rTCL
4
, and TCL
4
approach ground state to precision
O(\lambda^{2})
regardless of the qubit Hamiltonian. But, in the Ohmic bath at positive temperature, rTCL
4
will exhibit return to equilibrium to precision
O(\lambda^{2})
, only when the diagonal elements of
A
are zero.

Appendix I
Förster Regime

We will obtain the Förster rate by mapping to the exciton-in-a-dimer Hamiltonian:

H_{T}=-\frac{\Delta}{2}|1\rangle\langle 1|+\frac{\Delta}{2}|2\rangle\langle 2|+U(|1\rangle\langle 2|+|2\rangle\langle 1|)+|1\rangle\langle 1|\otimes F_{1}+|2\rangle\langle 2|\otimes F_{2}+H_{B1}+H_{B2}.

(229)

Each site is coupled to a local bath
characterized by the coupling and bath Hamiltonians
F_{i}
and
H_{Bi}
, respectively given by Eqs.
6
and
9
.
The baths are independent and the frequencies and form factors of the bath satisfy
\omega_{k1}=\omega_{k2}=\tilde{\omega}_{k}
and
g_{k1}=g_{k2}=\tilde{g}_{k}
.
The reorganization energy in each bath is

\tilde{E}_{r}=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega\,\frac{\tilde{J}_{\omega}}{\pi\omega}=-\tilde{S}_{0}.

(230)

The Förster transfer rate is computed treating the system energy
U
as perturbation, while the reference states are the asymptotic states of the pure-dephasing Hamiltonian. That is, for the exciton at each site, the bath state is taken to be the fully displaced thermal state. The Försters master equation is the Pauli equation in the site or the pointer basis, in which the coherence dynamics is traced out (as opposed to neglected
[
98
]
).
The Förster’s rate is the tunneling rate between sites, obtained in the limit
U\ll\Delta,\tilde{E}_{r}
, using
Ref.
[
90
, Eqs. 18, 21, 22]
:

R_{nn,mm}^{FRET}=2U^{2}\text{Re}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i\omega_{mn}t-2i\tilde{E}_{r}t-2g(t)}

(231)

where
g(t)
is the lineshape broadening function

\displaystyle g(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}dt_{1}\tilde{C}_{\beta}(t_{2})dt_{2}=\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\beta,0}(t_{1})

(232)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}\big[\tilde{J}_{\beta,0}(t_{1})+i\tilde{S}_{0}(t_{1})\big],

(233)

since
\tilde{S}_{\beta,0}(t)
is independent of
\beta
via Eq.
17
.
Thus,

R_{nn,mm}^{FRET}=2U^{2}\text{Re}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i\omega_{mn}t+2i\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}[\tilde{S}_{0}-\tilde{S}_{0}(t_{1})]-2\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}\,\tilde{J}_{\beta,0}(t_{1})}.

(234)

The relaxation rate is
\omega_{F}=R_{11,22}^{FRET}+R_{22,11}^{FRET}
,while the asymptotic site population is
\rho_{11}=R_{11,22}^{FRET}/\omega_{R}
.

The mapping to the qubit is done in two simple steps.
First, the hopping Hamiltonian
is mapped into a qubit Hamiltonian by the unitary transformation in the baths

\displaystyle b_{k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(b_{k1}-b_{k2})

(235)

\displaystyle b_{k}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(b_{k1}+b_{k2}).

(236)

Only the
b_{k}^{\prime}
-bath is coupled to the system, in terms of which the Hamiltonian is:

H^{\prime}=-\frac{\Delta}{2}\sigma_{z}+U\sigma_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{z}\otimes\sum_{k}\sqrt{2}\tilde{g}_{k}(b_{k}^{\prime}+b_{k}^{\prime\dagger})+\sum_{k}\tilde{\omega}_{k}b_{k}^{\prime\dagger}b_{k}^{\prime}.

(237)

Here, the Pauli matrices operate in the site basis, with
\sigma_{z}=\pm 1
corresponding to different sites. TCL
2n
is expressed in the eigenbasis of the isolated system Hamiltonian.
Next, we make the unitary transformation
W
into the isolated system energy eigenbasis. In the limit
U\ll\Delta
,
we have

W=\begin{pmatrix}1&U/\Delta\\
-U/\Delta&1\end{pmatrix},

(238)

and the Hamiltonian

H^{\prime\prime}=W^{\dagger}H^{\prime}W\approx-\frac{\Delta}{2}\sigma_{z}+A^{\prime}\otimes F^{\prime}+\sum_{k}\tilde{\omega}_{k}b_{k}^{\prime\dagger}b_{k}^{\prime},

(239)

where
F^{\prime}=\sum_{k}\tilde{g}_{k}(b_{k}^{\prime}+b_{k}^{\prime\dagger})
and

A^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&2U/\Delta\\
2U/\Delta&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(240)

This can be identified with the qubit Hamiltonian by scaling, e.g.
A=cA^{\prime}
,
g_{k}=\tilde{g}_{k}/c
and
\omega_{k}=\tilde{\omega}_{k}
.
Thus,
c=\sqrt{2}A_{11}
,
U=\Delta A_{12}/(2A_{11})
, and
\tilde{J}=c^{2}J=2A_{11}^{2}J
,
\tilde{S}=c^{2}S=2A_{11}^{2}S
.
Substituting into Eq.
234
leads to Eq.
162
, while in the limit
U\ll\Delta
, the populations in the site and isolated system energy eigenbasis are approximately equal.
